. Here,
\{\begin{subarray}{c}i\\
jk\end{subarray}\}
denote the Christoffel symbols of the spatial metric
\mathchoice{l^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{l^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{l^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{l^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}
and
\mathchoice{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}=-\mathchoice{a^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{a^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{a^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{a^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}=-\mathchoice{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}\varphi
is the negative acceleration of the stationary Killing orbits.
This equation shows that massive particles that are initially at rest in the considered reference frame experience an acceleration that is equal to
\mathchoice{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}
. Note that, despite this close analogy to Newtonian gravity,
Eq.

13
is an exact result that holds in arbitrary stationary space-times.
As such, this equation generally does not admit closed-form solutions, implying that exact or approximate solutions for specific experiments must be computed on a case-by-case basis.
Explicit expressions are discussed in
Sections

V
and
VI
.

IV
Detection Probabilities from Phase Shifts

The methods outlined in
Section

III
describe the deterministic evolution of the phase function
\Psi
.
While the phase is generally considered as being not directly observable,
phase differences
arising from interfering rays determine detection probabilities. This section summarizes the main equations relevant to the description of interferometric gravimeters and gradiometers using single-particle and multi-particle states following either bosonic or fermionic statistics.

For a general interferometer with
N_{\text{in}}
inputs and
N_{\text{out}}
outputs, let
\ket{\text{in}_{i}}
denote a (normalized) single-particle excitation of the input mode
i
. In the Heisenberg picture, this state
equals
a superposition of single-particle excitations
\ket{\text{out}_{k|i}}
of the various output modes
k
. Modeling potential losses using an additional “fictitious output port” that remains undetected, one thus has

\displaystyle\ket{\text{in}_{i}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{out}}}\ket{\text{out}_{k|i}}+\ket{\text{loss}_{i}}\,.

(14)

The corresponding equation for ladder operators takes the form

\displaystyle\mathchoice{a^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{i}}}}{a^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{i}}}}{a^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{i}}}}{a^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{i}}}}=\sum_{k}\mathchoice{b^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{k}{|}{i}}}}{b^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{k}{|}{i}}}}{b^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{k}{|}{i}}}}{b^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{k}{|}{i}}}}+\mathchoice{c^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{i}}}}{c^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{i}}}}{c^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{i}}}}{c^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{i}}}}\,.

(15)

By construction, the input vectors
\ket{\text{in}_{i}}
are orthonormal, and the output vectors satisfy
\braket{\text{out}_{k|i}\mid\text{out}_{l|j}}=0
for
k\neq l
since excitations at different outputs are perfectly distinguishable. For
k=l
, however, the inner products

\displaystyle\mathchoice{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{k}{|}{i}{j}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{k}{|}{i}{j}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{k}{|}{i}{j}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{k}{|}{i}{j}}}}

\displaystyle=\braket{\text{out}_{k|i}\mid\text{out}_{k|j}}\equiv\braket{0|\mathchoice{b_{{{k}{|}{i}}}}{b_{{{k}{|}{i}}}}{b_{{{k}{|}{i}}}}{b_{{{k}{|}{i}}}}\mathchoice{b^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{k}{|}{j}}}}{b^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{k}{|}{j}}}}{b^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{k}{|}{j}}}}{b^{{{\dagger}}}_{{{k}{|}{j}}}}|0}

(16)

have a non-trivial structure that depends on the details of the interferometer.
A simple model of a Mach–Zehnder interferometer with symmetric
50:50
beam splitters and equal losses in both arms is obtained by setting

\displaystyle(\mathchoice{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1}{|}{i}{j}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1}{|}{i}{j}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\make \displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}\begin{pmatrix}1-\mathscr{V}\cos\Delta\Psi&\phantom{1}+\mathscr{V}\sin\Delta\Psi\\
\phantom{1}+\mathscr{V}\sin\Delta\Psi&1+\mathscr{V}\cos\Delta\Psi\end{pmatrix}\,,

(17a)

\displaystyle(\mathchoice{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}{|}{i}{j}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}{|}{i}{j}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\make \displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}\begin{pmatrix}1+\mathscr{V}\cos\Delta\Psi&\phantom{1}-\mathscr{V}\sin\Delta\Psi\\
\phantom{1}-\mathscr{V}\sin\Delta\Psi&1+\mathscr{V}\cos\Delta\Psi\end{pmatrix}\,,

(17b)

where
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{V}
are parameters describing the overall transmission probability and the fringe visibility, respectively, and
\Delta\Psi
takes the form

\displaystyle\Delta\Psi=\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}+\Delta\Psi_{\xi}+\Delta\Psi_{*}\,,

(18)

where
\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}
and
\Delta\Psi_{\xi}
depend, respectively, only on the lapse and the shift along the rays
\gamma_{\textsc{i}}
and
\gamma_{\textsc{ii}}
as

\displaystyle\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}

\displaystyle=\int_{\gamma_{\text{I}}}\mathchoice{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}\mathchoice{\hat{n}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\hat{n}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\hat{n}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\hat{n}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}\mathchoice{\,\mathrm{d}x^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\,\mathrm{d}x^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\s (19a)

\displaystyle\Delta\Psi_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{*}}{c}\int_{\gamma_{*}}\mathchoice{\xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{d}x^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathrm{d}x^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathrm{d}x^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathrm{d}x^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,.

(19b)

Here, the wave vectors
\mathchoice{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}
and
\mathchoice{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.94443pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.94443pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.94443pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{k^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.94443pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}
depend on the dispersion relation, see
Eqs.

11
and
12
, and
\gamma_{*}
denotes the closed path obtained by concatenating
\gamma_{\text{I}}
with the reverse of
\gamma_{\text{II}}
.
[If
\gamma_{*}
encloses an area
A
, Stokes’ theorem implies
\Delta\Psi_{\xi}=\int_{A}\omega\,\mathchoice{\Xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{\Xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{\Xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{\Xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}\mathchoice{\mathrm{d}x^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathrm{d}x^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathrm{d}x^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][ .]
Finally,
\Delta\Psi_{*}
accounts for all additional phase shifts arising, for example, from optical mirrors and phase shifters (which are commonly used to introduce adjustable bias phases) or from the interaction of massive particles with matter or electromagnetic radiation
Peters et al. 2001
. Explicit expressions for
\Delta\Psi
depend on the experiment under consideration, see
Sections

V
and
VI
below.

For both bosonic and fermionic particles, the probability of detecting one particle at output no. 1 after it being injected into input no. 1 is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}[1-\mathscr{V}\cos(\Delta\Psi)]\,.

(20)

The coincide probability of detecting one particle at each of the outputs after two identical particles have been injected at the two inputs depends on the statistics:

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{2}^{\text{bos.}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}^{2}[1+\mathscr{V}^{2}\cos(2\Delta\Psi)]\,,

(21a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{2}^{\text{ferm.}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}^{2}[1+\mathscr{V}^{2}]\,.

(21b)

In general, gravity influences both the phase shift
\Delta\Psi
and the visibility
\mathscr{V}
.
However, since the former is typically more sensitive to gravitational effects than the latter
Zych et al. 2012
;
Mieling et al. 2022
;
Mieling 2023
, the following analysis focuses on
\Delta\Psi
and treats
\mathscr{V}
as a constant.

Equation

19
shows that, in the current model, all detection probabilities are expressible in terms of integrals of the lapse and shift along rays. Since other geometric quantities such as the curvature tensor
\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}{\rho}{\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}{\rho}{\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}{\rho}{\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}{\rho}{\sigma}}}}
do not enter explicitly, information about them can only be obtained by indirect means, e.g., using
Eq.

4
.
This absence of a direct curvature coupling is the result of the
wkb
approximation underlying the calculations of
Section

III
: more detailed analyses in Refs.
Exirifard et al. 2021
;
Oancea et al. 2020
;
Mieling and Hudelist 2025
show that Maxwell’s equations imply curvature-induced corrections to the dispersion relation, but such higher-order corrections are inaccessible to current experiments and are thus beyond the scope of this paper.

Having laid out the general methods for computing detection probabilities for quantum interferometers in a gravitational field,
Sections

V
and
VI
describe concrete experiments involving massless and massive particles, respectively.

V
Optical Experiments

This section does not follow the chronological development of the subject but rather present various experimental proposals sorted according to the mathematical complexity of their models.
For simplicity, the calculations presented here mainly focus on
static
frames of reference and treat shift-induced effect perturbatively.

V.1
Pound–Rebka Experiment

In their 1960 experiment, Pound and Rebka determined the gravitational redshift in γ-rays (that were emitted in the decay of
57
Co to
57
Fe) by measuring the difference in frequency over a height difference of
h=$74\text{\,}\mathrm{ft}$\approx$22.6\text{\,}\mathrm{m}$

Pound and Rebka 1960
. The result was found to be compatible with the theoretical prediction

\displaystyle\begin{split}z&=\frac{\nu_{h}-\nu_{0}}{\nu_{0}}\approx\Delta\varphi_{0\,h}/c^{2}\\
&\approx-\varg h/c^{2}\approx$-2.46\text{\times}{10}^{-15}$\,,\end{split}

(22)

which is a direct application of
Eq.

9
.
Since this experiment measured the shift in γ-ray frequencies relative to nuclear transition frequencies
Okun et al. 2000
, a debate arose whether the redshift is to be interpreted as a gravitational effect on light—as suggested by the paper title “Apparent weight of photons”
Pound and Rebka 1960
—or a gravitational effect on the emitting and absorbing atoms—as suggested by the title of the follow-up paper “Effect of gravity on nuclear resonance” by Pound and Snider
Pound and Snider 1964
—see, e.g., Ref.
Okun et al. 2000
and references therein.
Note, however, that the current definition of the second
Bureau international des poids et mesures 2018
fixes the frequency of light emitted in the ground-state hyperfine transition of
133
Cs. Consequently, in a (hypothetical) variant of the Pound–Rebka experiment involving such radiation, gravitational effects on the emission frequencies of the atoms are excluded
by definition
, thus leading to the alternative description of the redshift as a gravitational effect on light propagation.

The prevalent view that the Pound–Rebka experiment can be described—on the whole or at least in its central aspects—classically, i.e., without quantum theory
Zych et al. 2012
;
Brodutch et al. 2015
;
Zych et al. 2016
;
Aspelmeyer et al. 2017
;
Wu et al. 2024
;
Pallister et al. 2017
, has motivated the development of alternative experiments that could distinguish more clearly between the predictions of quantum optics in curved space-times and its classical or flat limits.

V.2
Tanaka’s Proposal

In his 1983 paper, Tanaka suggested using a fiber-optic Mach–Zehnder interferometer for measuring a phase shift that is induced on light by the gravitational redshift
Tanaka 1983
. A simplified schematic representation of such an interferometer is provided in
Fig.

2
.
In this setup, a coherent beam of light is injected on the left and split into two beams that traverse fiber-optic spools at different heights (the two spatial trajectories are denoted by
\gamma_{\textsc{i}}
and
\gamma_{\textsc{ii}}
, respectively), after which the rays interfere at a fiber-coupler before being detected. Rotating the interferometer about the horizontal allows modulating the gravitational phase difference between the two rays following the prescribed trajectories.

Figure 2:

Simplified schematic of the fiber-optic interferometer proposed in Ref.
Tanaka 1983
where a light ray is split into two paths that traverse fiber spools of equal length. The resulting phase shift at the output depends on the vertical separation between the two fiber spools via the redshift.

Based on
Eqs.

10
and
11
, one finds the lapse-induced phase shift for stationary solutions to be given by

\displaystyle\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}

\displaystyle=\frac{n\omega_{*}}{c}\left[\int_{\gamma_{\textsc{i}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}l}{\zeta}-\int_{\gamma_{\textsc{ii}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}l}{\zeta}\right]\,,

(23)

where
\omega_{*}
is the invariant Killing frequency.
This ray-optics result agrees with more rigorous fiber-optics calculations up to higher-order curvature corrections that are negligible in most applications
Beig et al. 2018
;
Mieling 2022
;
Mieling and Hudelist 2025
.
If both fiber spools have equal length
l
and the lapse is approximately constant along each of the two spools, the phase shift
\Delta\Psi_{\textsc{t}}
arising from small vertical separations
h
can be approximated by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta\Psi_{\textsc{t}}&\approx-\varg hln\omega/c^{3}\\
&\approx-${10}^{-6}\text{\,}\mathrm{rad}$\,(h/$\mathrm{m}$)\,(l/$\mathrm{km}$)\,(\lambda_{0}/$\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro m}$)^{-1}\,,\end{split}

(24)

where
n\approx 1.5
is the refractive index of glass and
\lambda_{0}=2\pi c/\omega
is the wavelength of light in vacuum, see also Ref.
Hilweg et al. 2017
.

After Ref.
Tanaka 1983
originally suggested performing the experiment with continuous-wave laser output, more recent papers suggested using single photons, pairs of entangled photons, and other non-classical states of light instead
Hilweg et al. 2017
;
Chen and Ralph 2019
;
Mieling 2022
;
Mieling et al. 2022
.
With appropriately chosen bias phases [specifically
\pi/(2N)
for
N
-photon N00N-states], and if the Sagnac effect is negligible, the single-photon and two-photon detection probabilities take the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}

\displaystyle\approx\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}[1-\mathscr{V}\varg hln\omega/c^{3}]\,,

(25a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{2}

\displaystyle\approx\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}[1+2\mathscr{V}^{2}\varg hln\omega/c^{3}]\,.

(25b)

Whereas the single-photon detection probabilities are identical to classical field intensities
Barnett 2022
, the non-classical factor of two in
\mathrm{P}_{2}
makes the two-photon coincidence experiment immune to such criticism.
As a consequence, two-photon interferometry experiments of this kind could demonstrate gravitationally induced quantum interference without a classical analog.

In Ref.
Tanaka 1983
, such an experiment was interpreted as a “twin paradox for photons.”
However, contrary to the standard “twin paradox,” the rays interfering at the second fiber coupler (where the world-lines intersect)
do not
pass the first fiber coupler at the same time. Indeed, the phase shift given above is fully attributable to such a temporal offset
Zych et al. 2012
;
Mieling 2023
. This is consistent with
Eq.

7a
, which shows that the rays acquire no gravitational phases while propagating through the fibers.

While the formulas given here directly apply to static frames of references, their use in stationary frames (where
\mathchoice{\xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\xi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}\neq 0
) requires some caution because the shift-induced phase difference
\Delta\Psi_{\xi}
is generally non-negligible. Using
Eq.

19
the ratio
|\Delta\Psi_{\xi}/\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}|
can be estimated by
2(\cos\vartheta)cA\Omega/(\varg lh)
, where
A
is the effective area of the surface enclosed by the interferometer and
\vartheta
is the angle between the corresponding surface normal and the local rotation vector
\mathchoice{\Omega^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Omega^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Omega^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Omega^{{{i}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
.
This shows that, on Earth, the Sagnac phase shift exceeds the gravitationally induced one by a factor of about
$4\text{\times}{10}^{3}$(\cos\vartheta)A/(lh)
. As a consequence, the isolation of
\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}
poses a severe experimental challenge. (For recent experiments on the Sagnac effect in quantum fiber-interferometry, see, e.g., Refs.
Bertocchi et al. 2006
;
Toroš et al. 2022
;
Cromb et al. 2023
;
Silvestri et al. 2024
. Their detailed description, however, is beyond the scope of this paper).

V.3
Stodolsky’s Proposal

In his 1979 paper, Stodolsky proposed a free-space Mach–Zehnder interferometer for measuring the gravitationally induced phase shift on light
Stodolsky 1979
(this was presented as an optical variant of the experiment done by Colella, Overhauser, and Werner, which is described in
Section

VI.1
below, and predates Tanaka’s proposal described above).
A simplified schematic of the setup is provided in
Fig.

3
. In contrast to Tanaka’s setup, light is not guided through optical fibers, but follows free-fall trajectories between the beam splitters and the mirrors.

(a)

(b)

Figure 3:

Schematic representation of optical free-fall trajectories in a Mach–Zehnder interferometer, with dots marking which points along the two rays reach the point
D
simultaneously.
If the system is inertial, interfering rays at
D
originate from the same point on the beam splitter
A
, which they pass at the same time (a).
This no longer applies in uniformly accelerated interferometers, as is the case in gravimeters (b).
Instead, the interfering rays must have a spatial and temporal offset at the first beam splitter
A
in order to reach the final beam splitter at the same space-time event.
The gray area indicates the minimal beam width required to observe the gravitationally induced phase shift.

Explicit integration of the geodesic equation (
13
) with constant
\mathchoice{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}{\varg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}}}}
shows that the rays interfering at the second beam splitter (
D
) pass the first beam splitter (
A
) at different times and different positions. To leading order in the acceleration
\varg
, these offsets are given by

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\textsc{s}}

\displaystyle=-(\varg hl/c^{3})(\cot^{2}\theta-1)\,,

(26a)

\displaystyle\Delta x_{\textsc{s}}

\displaystyle=-(\varg hl/c^{2})\cot^{2}\theta\,,

(26b)

\displaystyle\Delta z_{\textsc{s}}

\displaystyle=-(\varg hl/c^{2})\cot\theta\,,

(26c)

where
\theta
is the inclination of the mirrors and beam splitters that are separated horizontally by the length
l
and vertically by the height
h
.
Here and in the following,
\mathchoice{\Delta x^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Delta x^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Delta x^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Delta x^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\mathchoice{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}-\mathchoice{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}
refers to the coordinate separation between the upper ray
\mathchoice{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.2639pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.36111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.97221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}
(
ABD
) and the lower ray
\mathchoice{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.94443pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.94443pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.94443pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.5278pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72223pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.94443pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}
(
ACD
) at the respective events of entering the interferometer
2
2

2

The coordinate displacement arising here can be formalized geometrically using the notion of geodesic separation vectors, but this is not necessary for the purpose of computing the difference in phase between the two events with coordinates
\mathchoice{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}
and
\mathchoice{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{x^{{{\mu}}}_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}
using
Eq.

6
.
.
Since the phases are constant along the rays, the phase shift at
D
is given by the “separation phase”
\Psi(\mathchoice{x_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{x_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{x_{{{\textsc{i}}}}}{x_{{{\textsc{i}}}}})-\Psi(\mathchoice{x_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{x_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{x_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}}{x_{{{\textsc{ii}}}}})\approx\mathchoice{K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}\mathchoice{\Delta x^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Delta x^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Delta x^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Delta x^{{{\mu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
, see
Eq.

6
. Using
(\mathchoice{K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}}{K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}}}})\approx(-\omega,0,\omega/c,0)
, the lapse-induced phase shift thus takes the form

\displaystyle\Delta\Psi_{\textsc{s}}

\displaystyle\approx-\varg hl\omega/c^{3}\,,

(27)

which is identical to the phase shift in Tanaka’s setup with
n=1
. Hence, even though the two setups differ in the details of light propagation (guided motion within optical fibers is generally not geodesic), the resulting phase shifts and detection probabilities coincide.

The point of view that
geodesic motion
of light in the interferometer plays no essential role is supported by comparison with the analysis of Ref.
Zych et al. 2012
.
The rays considered there are lightlike curves of constant radial coordinate
r
in the Schwarzschild chart. Such rays fail to be geodesic unless
r=3GM/c^{2}

(
Wald 1984
, Sect. 6.3)
, a condition that rules out the comparison of two such rays at different
r
and is impossible to satisfy on Earth (the photon sphere of the Schwarzschild geometry with
M
equal to Earth’s mass would lie at
r\approx$1.3\text{\,}\mathrm{cm}$
).
Nonetheless, the resulting phase shift agrees with
Eq.

26
to the considered level of approximation.
However, these observations do not imply that the geometric details of the interferometer are wholly irrelevant.
Indeed, as shown in Appendix
A
, imbalances in the arm lengths can significantly alter the phase shift. For example, the assumption that the interferometer arms have equal
coordinate lengths
in the frequently used post-Newtonian gauge corresponds to having unequal
physical lengths
and hence leads to deviations from
Eq.

27
, see also Ref.
Brodutch et al. 2015
.

Similarly to Tanaka’s setup, the relative size of the shift-induced phase difference can be estimated by the ratio
2(\cos\vartheta)c\Omega/g\approx$4\text{\times}{10}^{3}$\cos\vartheta
. The isolation of the lapse-induced phase shift thus requires special alignments relative to the local rotation axis or alternative compensation or subtraction schemes.

V.4
Proposed Satellite Experiments

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 4:

Schematic representation of satellite experiments to measure the gravitationally induced phase shift on light.
The setup
4a
is a large-scale version of Tanaka’s setup (
Section

V.2
) and requires two uplinks.
The setups
4b
and
4c
, on the other hand, use a scheme similar to time-bin-encoding for which a single uplink suffices.
All these setups, however, yield the same lapse-induced phase shift.

In 2012, Rideout
et al
. proposed measuring the gravitationally induced phase shift on light in a satellite experiment
Rideout et al. 2012
. Subsequent papers described variants of this proposal
Mieling et al. 2022
;
Wu et al. 2024
;
Terno et al. 2020
;
Mohageg et al. 2022
;
Mieling et al. 2022
;
Mieling 2022
;
Terno et al. 2023
;
Bose et al. 2025
;
Lu et al. 2022
;
Wu et al. 2024
, whose setups are sketched in
Fig.

4
.
In all considered cases, the lapse-induced phase shift takes the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}&=\frac{n\omega_{*}l}{c}\left[\frac{1}{\zeta_{\text{s}}}-\frac{1}{\zeta_{\text{g}}}\right]\\
&=nl\omega_{\text{g}}z_{\text{g}\text{s}}/c\approx nl\omega_{\text{g}}(\varphi_{\text{g}}-\varphi_{\text{s}})/c^{3}\,,\end{split}

(28)

where
n
is the refractive index of the fiber-optic delay lines in the setups
4a
and
4b
, and
n=1
in the setup
4c
.
The parameter
l
denotes the length of the delay lines,
\omega_{\text{g}}
denotes the optical frequency at the ground station, and
z_{\text{g}\text{s}}
is the redshift factor
Rideout et al. 2012
;
Mohageg et al. 2022
;
Mieling et al. 2022
;
Wu et al. 2024
;
Bose et al. 2025
.
However, the lapse-induced phase shift is not the only relevant phase contribution in satellite experiments
Vallone et al. 2016
.
Indeed, in setups of the type
4a
the large area enclosed by the interferometer produces a significant shift-induced phase difference
\Delta\Psi_{\xi}
, and, moreover, in all setups shown in
Fig.

4
the satellite’s motion gives rise to an additional phase shift
\Delta\Psi_{\textsc{d}}
via the Doppler effect
Terno et al. 2020
.
Future implementations of such experimental setups thus require compensation schemes to isolate
\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}

Terno et al. 2023
;
Wu et al. 2024
.

V.5
Main Experimental Challenges

All optical experiments described above ultimately depend on the magnitude of the redshift as given by
Eq.

9
. A direct observation of this effect with single photons requires either a large height difference between the source and receiver, or the use of short wavelengths, as was done in the Pound–Rebka experiment. For example, at telecommunication wavelengths (
1550\text{\,}\mathrm{nm}
) the absolute value of the gravitational frequency shift is about
18\,000
times smaller than that for the γ-rays used in the Pound–Rebka experiment (
86\text{\,}\mathrm{pm}
). In interferometric experiments using single- or multi-photon states, the gravitational redshift manifests itself as a phase shift between the paths at different altitudes. By extending the path lengths, for example using optical fibers, this phase shift becomes detectable even at telecom wavelengths and laboratory-scale height differences
Zych et al. 2012
;
Hilweg et al. 2017
.

A key experimental challenge is the isolation of the gravitational signal from phase noise introduced by the light-guiding medium. Any differential change in the optical path length leads to phase shifts that cannot be distinguished from the signal of interest within the same frequency band. To stabilize the interferometer arms, a coherent light source at a frequency distinct from that of the photons in the quantum state can be used. This wavelength-division multiplexing method allows for two different approaches:

(i)

The stabilization laser can be used to maintain a constant phase difference between the two arms, regardless of their vertical separation. This approach compensates for the gravitational redshift experienced by the laser and thus only allows to bound any potential difference in the gravitationally induced phase shifts between the laser and the single- or multi-photon quantum states, i.e., the quantity
\epsilon=\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}^{\text{laser}}-\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}^{\text{photon}}
. (Note, however, that the theoretical prediction
\epsilon=0
is independent of the value of
\varg
and, moreover, does not depend on the validity of Einstein’s field equations.)

(ii)

Alternatively, after initial calibration, the two interferometer arms can be stabilized independently. This enables direct measurements of the lapse-induced phase shift of the quantum states as a function of height, assuming sufficient phase stability in both arms independently.

Independent stabilization places severe bounds on laser stability and accuracy. This can be exemplified by the satellite experiments using time-bin encoding shown in
Figs.

4b
and
4c
. The first constraint arises because different lasers are used to stabilize the two unbalanced interferometers, one at the ground station and one on the satellite. The difference in their central frequencies,
\Delta\nu
, must not exceed the gravitational redshift on the signal photons,
|\Delta\nu|<\nu_{0}|\Delta\varphi|/c^{2}
. For experiments on Earth, this is a severe constraint as it leads to an admissible difference in laser frequencies of only a few tens of
\mathrm{mHz}
per meter of height difference while for satellite experiments the acceptable difference relaxes to several
\mathrm{kHz}
. The second constraint arises from the wavelength differences between the stabilization lasers and the photons whose redshift is to be measured. In each unbalanced Mach–Zehnder interferometer, the induced phase difference between the stabilization laser and the quantum-state photons varies with the arm length difference
\delta l
between the long arm common to both rays and the short reference arm. Any difference in length imbalance between the ground (
\delta l_{\text{g}}
) and the satellite station (
\delta l_{\text{s}}
) leads to a phase shift between the interfering time-bin amplitudes and must be smaller than the gravitational phase,
2\pi n|\nu_{0}-\nu_{\text{laser}}|\,|\delta l_{\text{s}}-\delta l_{\text{s}}|/c<|\Delta\Psi_{\zeta}|
. This constraint is much less demanding than the one mentioned above, but still requires attention for earthbound experiments. For
10\text{\,}\mathrm{km}
-long fiber spools separated in altitude by
20\text{\,}\mathrm{m}
, the frequency difference between telecom-wavelength photons and the laser should be smaller than about
4\text{\,}\mathrm{MHz}
for
|\delta l_{\text{g}}-\delta l_{\text{s}}|=$1\text{\,}\mathrm{mm}$
. Since this is too narrow for filtering, the stabilization laser needs to be injected from the other end of the interferometer.

Although optical fibers allow for a comparatively simple way of increasing the interferometric area by extending the optical path length in each arm, they also introduce significant challenges. Losses in the fibers and other optical components can severely limit the number of detected photons and thus the precision in the estimation of the phase shift due to shot noise. High losses thus increase the duration of the measurement and therefore the stability requirements on the experiments. The simultaneous use of quantum states and stabilization lasers within the same optical fiber can also introduce noise via several mechanisms
Hilweg et al. 2022
. Spontaneous Raman scattering of photons from the laser into the quantum channel and fundamental thermal noise are among the most difficult to deal with. A potential solution is to use (doubly) nested anti-resonant nodeless fibers (
dnanf
)
Poletti 2014
instead of standard single mode fibers. In
dnanf
, more than
99\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
of the optical mode can propagate in vacuum with greatly reduced losses
Chen et al. 2024
and non-linear effects
Liu et al. 2019
.

Several methods exist to separate the lapse-induced phase from phase shifts induced by the Sagnac effect. The conceptually simplest approach is to orient the interferometer such that its surface normal is aligned at an angle of
\vartheta=\pi/2
with respect to Earth’s rotation axis. While straightforward in principle, this configuration is only practical for small-scale, laboratory-based experiments. For schemes using laser stabilization to maintain a constant phase relationship between the two arms, the Sagnac effect is common to both the coherent state and the single- or multi-photon quantum states and thus does not contribute to the differential output signal. When optical fibers are used, they can be coiled so that the winding direction is reversed halfway through the fiber length. This configuration ensures that light in both halves of the fiber experiences equal and opposite phase shifts, resulting in a net-zero Sagnac contribution.

VI
Matter-Wave Experiments

Whereas gravitationally induced phase shifts have not yet been observed in optical experiments, analogous effects in matter-wave experiments have already been measured. This section describes the main characteristics of these experiments using the general methods developed in
Sections

III
and
IV
.
Similarly to the analyses in
Section

V
above, the calculations presented here mainly focus on static frames of references for simplicity of exposition.

VI.1
Colella–Overhauser–Werner Experiment

In their 1974 paper, Overhauser and Colella proposed a neutron-interferometry experiment for demonstrating gravitationally induced phase shifts
Overhauser and Colella 1974
, a simplified schematic of which is shown in
Fig.

5
.
In this setup, a neutron beam is split, reflected, and recombined by diffraction at three parallel slabs cut from a single crystal of silicon.
The expected phase shift in the Overhauser–Colella setup is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta\Psi_{\textsc{oc}}&=-\frac{\varg hl}{\hslash\varv^{2}}\frac{m\varv}{\sqrt{1-\varv^{2}/c^{2}}}\\
&=-\frac{\varg hlm}{\hslash\varv}\left[1+O(\varv^{2}/c^{2})\right]\,,\end{split}

(29)

where the height difference
h
depends on the base length
l
and the inclination angle
\theta
of the crystal slabs,
h=l\sin(2\theta)
, and
\varv
is the neutron’s velocity.
The leading-order term in
Eq.

29
was originally derived using the semiclassical approximation of the Schrödinger equation and assuming the neutron rays to be straight lines
Overhauser and Colella 1974
. Similar derivations of the phase shift using straight rays are given, for example, in Refs.
Anandan 1977
;
Kuroiwa et al. 1993
;
Varjú and Ryder 2000
;
Galiautdinov and Ryder 2017
;
Bose et al. 2025
.
In his 1998 paper, Mannheim showed that—by taking into account the bending of the rays under the influence of gravity—the phase shift can be explained by the fact that rays interfering at the output must have reached the central crystal slab at different times
Mannheim 1998
. Specifically, the temporal and spatial offsets of the rays shown in
Fig.

5b
are given by

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\textsc{oc}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varg hl}{\varv^{3}}\left[\cot^{2}\theta+1-2\frac{\varv^{2}}{c^{2}}\right]\,,

(30a)

\displaystyle\Delta x_{\textsc{oc}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varg hl}{\varv^{2}}\cot^{2}\theta\,,

(30b)

\displaystyle\Delta z_{\textsc{oc}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varg hl}{\varv^{2}}\cot\theta\,,

(30c)

as can be calculated by explicitly integrating the geodesic equation (
13
) if the spatio-temporal extent of the experiment is sufficiently small so that curvature terms are negligible.
In the limit
\varv\to c
, the offsets given in
Eq.

30
reproduce those for light stated in
Eq.

26
. Similarly, the phase shift
\Delta\Psi_{\textsc{oc}}
reproduces the optical result
\Delta\Psi_{\textsc{s}}=-ghl\omega/c
provided one replaces the relativistic particle momentum
mv/\sqrt{1-v^{2}/c^{2}}
with the optical analog
\hslash k=\hslash\omega/c
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 5:

Schematic representation of the neutron interferometer proposed by Overhauser and Colella in Ref.
Overhauser and Colella 1974
.
In the absence of gravity the rays form a rhombus (a), but in the presence of gravity rays interfering at
D
must have been injected into the interferometer at different heights (b).
The dots indicate which points along the two rays reach the point
D
simultaneously and the gray region shows the minimal beam width required to observe phase shifts in the presence of gravity.

In their 1975 paper, Colella, Overhauser, and Werner (
cow
) reported on the experimental observation of gravitationally induced phase shifts in single neutrons using a setup whose geometry differs from that originally proposed by Overhauser and Colella
Colella et al. 1975
, see
Fig.

6
for a simplified schematic.
Compared to the Overhauser–Colella setup shown in
Fig.

5
, the incoming rays are sent towards one of the outer crystal slabs, rather than the middle one. As a consequence, the free-fall trajectories in the Overhauser–Colella setup cross, but those in the
cow
setup do not, cf.
Figs.

5b
and
6b
.
Similarly to the Overhauser–Colella setup, the rays interfering at the output must have reached the first crystal slab at different times and at different locations:

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\textsc{cow}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varg hl}{\varv^{3}}\left[\tan^{2}\theta+1-2\frac{\varv^{2}}{c^{2}}\right]\,,

(31a)

\displaystyle\Delta x_{\textsc{cow}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varg hl}{\varv^{2}}\tan^{2}\theta\,,

(31b)

\displaystyle\Delta z_{\textsc{cow}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varg hl}{\varv^{2}}\tan\theta\,,

(31c)

cf.
Eq.

30
.
The overall phase shift, however, is identical to that of the previous setup:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta\Psi_{\textsc{cow}}&=-\frac{\varg hl}{\hslash\varv^{2}}\frac{m\varv}{\sqrt{1-\varv^{2}/c^{2}}}\\
&=-\frac{\varg hlm}{\hslash\varv}\left[1+O(\varv^{2}/c^{2})\right]\,.\end{split}

(32)

Similarly to the Overhauser–Colella phase shift, there are multiple derivations for
Eq.

32
based on various approximations, differing, in particular, in whether the rays are taken to be straight lines or free-fall parabolas, cf., e.g., Refs.
Beyer and Nitsch 1986
;
Bose et al. 2025
.

Equation

32
can be generalized to account for the non-zero thickness of the crystal slabs
Colella et al. 1975
;
Bonse and Wroblewski 1984
;
Horne 1986
but the detailed description of such non-gravitational effects is beyond the scope of this paper.

Contrary to the optical experiments described in
Sections

V.2
and
V.3
, the parameters entering
Eqs.

29
and
32
are not independent since the diffraction of the rays must satisfy the Bragg–Laue condition

\boldsymbol{n}\cdot(\boldsymbol{p}_{\text{out}}-\boldsymbol{p}_{\text{in}})=\hslash\,\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}_{\text{lattice}}
,
where
\boldsymbol{k}_{\text{lattice}}
is a reciprocal lattice vector and
\boldsymbol{n}
is a collinear unit vector
3
3

3

Specifically, if
d_{\text{lattice}}
denotes the lattice constant along
\boldsymbol{n}
,
\boldsymbol{k}_{\text{lattice}}
must be an integer multiple of
(2\pi/d_{\text{lattice}})\boldsymbol{n}
, with the absolute value of this integer coefficient being commonly referred to as the order of the diffraction process (the diffraction in the
cow
experiment was of first order). In practice, reflection also occurs for momenta that do not satisfy the diffraction condition
exactly
, with the finite range of admissible
\boldsymbol{p}_{\text{in}}
being known as the Darwin plateau.
.
Using this notation, for
\varv\ll c
,
Eq.

32
can be written in the form

\displaystyle\Delta\Psi_{\textsc{cow}}

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\varg}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}_{\text{lattice}}\,T_{*}^{2}\,,

(33)

where
T_{*}=l/\varv
is the time of uninterrupted free fall (up to negligible corrections of higher order), see also Ref.
(
Wolf et al. 2011
, App. A)
. The observation that the parameters
h
,
l
, and
m
in
Eqs.

32
and
32
are constrained to a two-dimensional parameter space resolves the tension between the statement that “the phase shifts in atomic beam and neutron interferometry in general do depend on the mass”
Lämmerzahl 1996
on the one hand, and that “the phase shift is independent of the mass of the atoms (neutrons) or the associated Compton frequency”
Wolf et al. 2011
on the other.

Similarly to the optical experiments described above, the relative size of the shift-induced phase difference can be estimated by the ratio
2(\cos\vartheta)\varv\Omega/\varg
. For the
cow
experiment, for example, this evaluates to
\sim$4\text{\times}{10}^{-2}$\cos\vartheta
. The reason for this suppression of shift-induced effects compared to optical setups lies in the velocity ratio
\varv/c\ll 1
. Hence, decreasing the particle’s velocity reduces the relative error produced by neglecting the shift-induced phase.

As shown in
Eqs.

11
and
12
, the gravitationally induced phase shift in neutrons is larger than that in photons by a factor that is given by the ratio of the optical wavelength to the neutron’s de Broglie wavelength. As a result, gravitational effects can be measured using interferometers with arm lengths of only a few centimeters
Littrell et al. 1997
, in contrast to the kilometer-scale interferometers required for optical experiments, as discussed above. However, current results from
cow
experiments on neutron interferometry can also be derived using a purely Newtonian gravitational description, and as such, do not provide further insight into the combined framework of quantum theory and general relativity. In order to measure effects indicative of general relativistic theory such as space-time curvature or Lense–Thirring precession, a much larger interaction area would be required, since the corrections from higher-order gravitational effects are on the order of
{10}^{-9}
of the
cow
phase
Anandan 1984
. Recent advancements in neutron interferometry include the fabrication of large, defect-free silicon crystals or use of other materials for neutron diffraction
Hadden et al. 2024
;
Huber et al. 2024
, very cold neutron sources
Mezei 2022
and improvements in environmental shielding and detection
Pushin et al. 2015
. While these are increasing the sensitivity of neutron interferometry, it is still limited by small interaction areas, pushing gravitational research with massive particles towards atom fountain experiments that allow for a much larger interaction length (see
Section

VI.2
). Alternative proposals include focusing on using the neutron’s spin precession in a magnetic field as a “clock” to track the proper time of the neutron’s evolution, and thus allowing measurement of the proper time difference across the gravitational field
Zych et al. 2011
. However, these effects would be minuscule (
\sim${10}^{-10}$
of the
cow
phase for a magnetic field of
10\text{\,}\mathrm{T}
), and are thus out of reach for current technologies. An in-depth discussion on possible neutron experiments involving gravity is provided in Ref.
(
Rauch and Werner 2015
, Chap. 8)
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 6:

Schematic representation of the neutron interferometer described by Colella, Overhauser, and Werner in Ref.
Colella et al. 1975
.
In the absence of gravity (a), the rays form a rhombus similar to that in the setup proposed by Overhauser and Colella, see
Fig.

5a
, but in the presence of gravity (b) the free-fall trajectories differ from those shown in
Fig.

5b
.
As a consequence, the minimal beam width (indicated by the gray band and the dots along the rays) and the offsets at the first beam splitter are reduced.
The overall phase shift, however, is the same in both setups.

VI.2
Atomic Fountains

Whereas the interferometers described above enclose non-zero areas, in atomic fountain experiments the particle motion is commonly approximated as being restricted to one spatial dimension
Peters et al. 2001
;
Overstreet et al. 2021
.
More generally, if motion takes place in the vicinity of a spatial curve, one can locally erect spatial Fermi coordinates
(x,y,z)\equiv(\mathchoice{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},z)
, relative to which the spatial metric
\mathchoice{l^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{l^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{l^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}{l^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{j}}}}
takes a particularly simple form
Mieling and Hudelist 2025
. If the reference curve is a spatial geodesic (as is relevant, for example, for radial motion in spherically symmetric geometries), one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}l&=[1-\mathchoice{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.49423pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.7816pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.49423pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.7816pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{z}{\alpha}{z}{\beta}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.49423pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.7816pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.49423pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.7816pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{z}{\alpha}{z}{\beta}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.49423pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.7816pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.49423pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.7816pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{z}{\alpha}{z}{\beta}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.49423pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.7816pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.10768pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.49423pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.7816pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{z}{\alpha}{z}{\beta}}}}\mathchoice{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{x^{{{\beta}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\beta}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\beta}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\beta}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}](\mathrm{d}z)^{2}\\
&\quad+[\mathchoice{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}}}}-\tfrac{1}{3}\mathchoice{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebo &\quad+O[(\mathchoice{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}{\beta}}}}\mathchoice{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{x^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\mak
